change and external shocks tend to exacerbate these inequalities. Multiple and entrenched barriers, which are
embedded in institutions, norms, attitudes and physical spaces, constrain their ability to fulfil their fundamental
rights including access to health care and other vital services.
Persons with disabilities, especially young persons with disabilities, often live on the margins of their community.
Families and communities may assume that they are unable to engage in mainstream activities such as attending
school which subsequently results in a lack of skills; low confidence, expectations and achievement; and limited
workplace opportunities. Many disabled women and children face a heightened risk of domestic and sexual violence.
However, opportunities do exist to strengthen the participation of persons with disabilities in local food systems and
pro-poor value chains.
PART II: GENDER AND SOCIAL INCLUSION ACTION PLAN
The programme has set a target to invest 50% of programme resources for women-led investments and
to investments generating employment for women, and for the advisory services to achieve a gender
balance among the end beneficiaries in service delivery. In addition, 30% of beneficiaries are to be the
youth.
The objective of the ARCAFIM GESI action plan is to ensure that women, youth and people with disabilities
have equal opportunity to access and benefit from CCA financing as men and to secure climate-resilient
livelihoods. The action plan has four principal audiences:
- end-users of CCA finances and ARCAFIM programme services: smallholders, MSMEs and their
communities, including women, youth and people with disabilities;
- financial service providers: Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) which will be offering CCA
financial services to the small producer and MSME markets, including host bank branches and
agent networks, micro-finance institutions (MFIs), savings and credit cooperatives (SACCOs) and
Fintechs;
- other service providers: such as advisory and extension staff, input suppliers, agribusinesses,
vale chain actors; and
- programme management: ARCAFIM Facility Coordination Unit at IFAD, PMU at the host bank
HQ and ARCAFIM focal points at country subsidiaries.
Purpose
The purpose of the ARCAFIM programme is to make rural climate change adaptation finance work for Africa’s future:
• To catalyse and scale up private sector climate change adaptation financing targeting smallholders and rural
and rural MSMEs in the agricultural sector in Africa.
• To address the environmental, technical, economic, social and political factors faced by smallholder
producers and rural MSMEs to adapt and build stronger resilience to climate change and thereby bring
about growth and opportunity.
Theory of change, assumptions and risks

=== PAGE 103 ===
Page 99
The GESI action plan is an integral part of the overall ARCAFIM. The programme will use the twin-track approach to
mainstream GESI considerations into ARCAFIM operations, complemented by targeted interventions to overcome
any specific barriers as necessary. This approach will enable programme opportunities to be expanded to women,
youth and persons with disabilities, while also addressing the main gaps and barriers they face to inclusion.
The GESI theory of change is founded on the need to identify and address social norms that perpetuate GESI
inequalities and exacerbate barriers on both the supply and demand sides of CCA financial inclusion. GESI activities
are present in programme component 2 (which addresses the non-financial barriers to CCA financing) and
programme implementation arrangements.
Early programme implementation arrangements will ensure there is sufficient capacity and skills to deliver and track
GESI performance through the recruitment of specialists, selection of implementing partners, strengthening capacity
of those responsible for programme delivery, and the monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) system.
Early GESI actions include conducting gender and social norms diagnostic studies at country and local levels to
better understand the context in which ARCAFIM will be operating. Gender, youth and disability lenses will be applied
understanding social norms, CCA technologies and practices, and financial products and services. These insights will
provide the foundation for stimulating all stakeholders (from programme staff, host agency, PFIs and service providers
through to community members and leaders) engage in critical reflection on norms that perpetuate inequalities
and stimulate positive behaviour change in the inclusion-climate-finance nexus.
As a result of capacity building for critical reflection and behaviour change on social inclusion issues, PFIs will view
women, youth and people with disabilities as potential eligible borrowers of CCA finance. Under sub-component 2.1,
PFIs will address supply-side barriers to CCA finance by seeking innovative ways to establish credit worthiness in the
absence of traditional collateral, providing relevant CCA products and services, and identifying digital and non-digital
routes to access services.
At the community, household and individual levels, the shift in gender norms and attitudes will create an enabling
environment in which women, youth and people with disabilities will be able to make independent decisions to
borrow money and adopt CCA technologies and practices. Under sub-component 2.2, the demand for CCA finance
will be strengthened by developing their climate adaptive knowledge and skills, financial literacy, entrepreneurship
and business skills.
Activities under sub-component 2.3 will further strengthen the enabling environment for inclusive climate finance at
the policy level.
The outcomes for women and youth as a result of accessing CCA finance will be securing climate change-resilient
livelihoods with the benefits of food and water security (including reduced workloads associated with household
tasks); improved business and employment opportunities and incomes; gains in voice and decision-making over the
use of assets and outputs, including income generated; and reductions in harmful practices and domestic violence.
The principal institutional gains will be the ability of the Host Bank and PFIs to expand the outreach of CCA finance
to individuals that have traditionally been excluded, and a more conducive policy environment.
These outcomes will feed into ARCAFIM outcome 3 (increased CC resilience), outcome 4 (improved food and water
security) and the co-benefits 1 (increased incomes) and 2 (employment generation).
The key assumptions that underpin the GESI pathway moving from actions to outputs and outcomes include:
stakeholders are willing to engage in the process of critical reflection to understand their unconscious biases and
adapt their behaviours accordingly; the PMU and implementing partners embrace the GESI agenda and ensure it is
reflected in resource allocation and tracking performance; there are sufficient skilled implementing partners and

=== PAGE 104 ===
Page 100
service providers to deliver high quality GESI activities; and policy makers are willing to take measures to improve
the policy frameworks to strengthen financial inclusion. Upstream and downstream linkages in the food system are
reasonably buoyant and small and medium enterprises provide opportunities for employment.
The risks of not addressing social inclusion issues in CCA finance include:
• Negative social norms would continue to disempower women, youth and people with disabilities, limiting
their livelihood opportunities and perpetuating harmful practices including violence.
• A significant proportion of the population would not have the ability, knowledge, skills or confidence to
access and use CCA finance to ensure their livelihoods to become climate change secure. In turn, this
would threaten the stability of rural communities and may trigger outmigration.
• PFIs would miss the opportunity to expand their client base.
Sub-component 2.1: Awareness raising and capacity development of PFIs for CCA finance
Under sub-component 2.1, the action plan will ensure that GESI considerations are mainstreamed into the supply
side of CCA finance work of the PFIs and other service providers (such as advisory services, value chain actors).
Capacity building for critical reflection and behaviour change among PFIs and other service providers:
This activity will follow on from the findings of gender and social norms diagnostic studies which will be conducted
in each country during early implementation (see implementation arrangements for further details). Using
participatory approaches to facilitate dialogue, trust, ownership, visioning and behaviour change at various levels,
frontline staff and management from PFIs, BDS and other service providers will critically reflect on deep-rooted social
and gender norms and attitudes which have an adverse effect on financial and other service provision to women,
youth and people with disabilities. This will help stimulate positive behaviour change and lay the foundation for PFI
staff to recognise the capacity of women and others to save and borrow money from the formal sector, and ensure
financial products and services are relevant to their needs.
Technical support for PFIs (host bank and local intermediaries):
• The CCA finance strategy (including CCA financial advisory capacities) and rural outreach and social
inclusion strategies will specifically address how the CCA products and services will developed and
disseminated to women, youth and people with disabilities. Methods for community engagement and
outreach will be inclusive and gender responsive. Women field staff may make it easier to engage with rural
women.
• CCA lending products and services (including climate risk insurance) will be tailored to the needs, resources
and capacities of women and youth who are small producers or micro/small entrepreneurs in the food
system.22 The range of products will cover production, post-harvest and value addition activities for both
household consumption and the market undertaken by smallholders and micro/small businesses. They will
also include energy-saving and climate smart technologies to make all aspects of rural livelihoods more
climate resilient and reduce the workload of household tasks (such as biomass production, improved stoves,
solar lanterns, water harvesting and storage systems, micro-irrigation for home gardens, potable water,
basic food processing).
22 Financial products could be co-developed between the FPIs and potential clients from different backgrounds to ensure their
relevance. For further details, see Mayoux, L and IFAD. 2017. Financial Action Learning System (FALS) An integrated
methodology for client financial empowerment and responsible financial service provision, Concept Note
https://gamechangenetwork.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/02/FALS_IFADConceptNote_Marchweb.pdf

=== PAGE 105 ===
Page 101
• The taxonomy framework for end-borrower investment eligibility will be reviewed regularly for relevance
and effectiveness from a GESI perspective. The framework will adopt a holistic livelihood approach and will
include technologies and practices that make both the productive and domestic aspects of rural livelihoods
more climate change resilient. Adjustments will be made as necessary to make the framework more
inclusive.
• Innovative ways will be used to establish creditworthiness and assess risks among borrowers that would
enable resource poor households (including those enterprises headed by women and youth) to gain access
to finance. Alternative forms of collateral could include group security or non-land assets.
• Repayment terms could be adjusted to meet the capacity of borrowers and enable them to adjust to
unexpected shocks, with longer grace periods and repayment periods, and options for pausing repayment
schedules without severe penalties.
• Insurance products will be tailored to reduce the risk of borrowing and protect agricultural investments
against climate risk. They will be made more accessible to rural women, youth and people with disabilities
by insurance providers adopting new approaches, such as flexible premium payments and bundling with
other services.
• Build the capacity of MFIs and SACCOs to support women, youth and people with disabilities in sustainable
livelihood development and agribusiness, as well as members and leaders of their organizations.
Identifying and piloting technology-enabled innovative solutions:
• Digital financial technologies will be used for cost-effective transactions, credit assessment and risk
management. Account will be taken of gender- and youth-differentiated access to digital services, through
device financing, agent networks, group ownership of tablets for accessing climate and extension
information (for example, by SACCOs or farmer field schools), e-vouchers etc.).
• Advisory packages, market information, weather and climate information will be integrated into the
mobile banking financial service interface. The content will be adapted to be relevant to the different needs
of the end-users, including women, youth and people with disabilities, and disseminated in local languages.
• Alternatives to digital services will be developed for those with limited access to such technologies, such
as community radios, information boards, public announcements on market days, village agents, mobile
services etc.
• Conduct market studies to identify market opportunities for commodities of interest to women, youth and
people with disabilities and support the growth of their enterprises.
Sub-component 2.2: CCA investment support services to small producers and rural MSMEs
Under sub-component 2.2, the action plan will ensure that GESI considerations are mainstreamed into the provision
of targeted and practical technical assistance to stimulate demand for CCA finance.
Capacity building for critical reflection and behaviour change at the community level: This activity will follow on
from the findings of gender and social norms diagnostic studies, which will be conducted in each country during
early implementation (see implementation arrangements for further details). Participatory approaches (see box 2)
will be used in the community to facilitate dialogue, trust, ownership, visioning and behaviour change with
individuals, households, groups, leaders and upstream and downstream value chain actors. They will critically reflect
on deep-rooted social and gender norms and attitudes in order to challenge power dynamics and bring about a
paradigm shift towards gender equality and social inclusion, specifically around financial inclusion and adapting

=== PAGE 106 ===
Page 102
livelihoods to climate change. Some activities will explicitly engage with norm holders, men and boys to address the
concepts of masculinity, gender and stigma.
Box 2: Gender Action Learning System (GALS)
GALS and household methodologies will be instrumental in enabling gender transformation amongst
smallholders in groups and in farmer groups to empower women and men towards financial inclusion, more
balanced workloads, equitable decision making and benefits sharing. GALS tools will enable households to
discuss the importance of climate-change adaptation and address the gender and social norms which could
hinder access to financial products and the adoption of CCA technologies and practices in both productive
work and household tasks.
With the exception of the Republic of Tanzania, the participating countries have service providers with
extensive experience of the GALS.
For further details about GALS, see IFAD. 2022. How to integrate the Gender Action Learning System (GALS)
in IFAD operations https://www.ifad.org/documents/38714170/45173373/htdn_gals.pdf/38ec05a9-d0a9-
3559-6306-d73b5db550dc?t=1648470620545
Small-scale agricultural producer CCA investment promotion and financial literacy services:
• Ensure the membership and leadership of the 16,000 rural producer groups to be trained include 50%
women and 30% youth (of whom 50% are young women).
• Promote inclusive leadership in governance structures such as community organizations, cooperatives,
farmer groups, VLSAs, SACCOs and MFIs through leadership training, mentoring and developing their role
as change agents.
• Through participatory engagement with the community, ensure CCA products and services meet the
priority needs and resources of different groups within the food system, including women, youth and
people with disabilities. Understand the differentiated impacts of climate change on rural livelihoods and
demand for CCA options. For example, women may prefer less risky but more labour-intensive climate
smart technologies if they can allocate their own labour and accrue the benefits for themselves.23 They
might prefer to invest in crops that contribute to household food and nutrition needs or new crop varieties
or supplement traditional revenue with diversified production activities.24 In addition, they will be
interested in technologies that will make the domestic aspects of rural livelihoods more climate change
resilient, such as water collection/harvesting, storage and use; fuel collection and use; food processing and
preparation; and energy. Consequently, they will have different needs for weather and climate
information, training and technology options based on their access to productive assets, social and gender
norms, and household division of labour.
• Conduct awareness campaigns about CCA technologies and practices to reach the widest rural audiences
through a variety of media, drama, demonstration plots, study visits and digital technologies. Include
women’s groups and local information-sharing mechanisms as communication channels.
23 Lambrecht, I., Bernard, V., and Miet, M .2016. Agricultural extension in Eastern Democratic Republic of Congo: does gender
matter? Eur. Rev. Agric. Econ. 43, 841–874 https://hdl.handle.net/10568/75754
24 World Bank, FAO, IFAD. 2015. Gender in Climate-Smart Agriculture. Washington, DC: World Bank; FAO; IFAD. Available online
at: http:// documents.worldbank.org/curated/en/654451468190785156/pdf/99505- REVISED-Box393228B-PUBLIC-Gender-
and-Climate-Smart-AG-WEB-3. pdf

=== PAGE 107 ===
Page 103
• Develop CCA skills through farmer field schools, demonstration plots, study visits. Provide opportunities for
women, youth and people with disabilities to experiment with the use of climate smart technologies and
practices, in particular to identify and address any potential increase in labour requirements or potential
risks.
• Train and sensitize input suppliers on the needs of women through the GALS household methodology,
enhancing the availability and relevance of the provided inputs.
Financial products and services:
• Develop and strengthen financial and digital literacy skills and savings culture among women and youth
including basic banking skills, the use of mobile and digital banking services, and decision making on savings,
borrowing money and using income earned.
• Encourage household members to identify and discuss gender norms and practices, which impact on their
attitudes towards borrowing, savings and prepare financial vision journeys.25
• Provide technical support to help develop CCA investment proposals through strengthening business,
entrepreneurship and negotiating skills, and introducing mentoring schemes as a pathway to upgrade and
expand participation by women, youth and people with disabilities in the food system.
Agribusiness CCA business development services26. Activities to strengthen the GESI focus of BDS to 625
agribusinesses include:
• Ensure business support organizations understand their GESI gaps and develop products and services that
are inclusive; for example, BDS training in small groups and individual consulting services is relevant and
accessible; market opportunities are accessible.
• Provide vocational training for young women and men to strengthen their potential both in the labour
market and as self-employed entrepreneurs, identify role models, use different training formats available
through the internet and mobile phones.
• Embed a GESI module in agrientrepreneur training27 to ensure the social dimensions of entrepreneurship
development are addressed.
• Ensure agribusiness employment opportunities provide quality jobs and promote the decent work agenda
with a fair income and is undertaken in conditions of freedom, equity, security and human dignity.
• Promote inclusive agribusiness development across the food system and establish market opportunities
for small producers; for example, by supporting farmer organizations and cooperatives, encouraging
collective marketing platforms, supporting value addition through agro-processing, promoting equitable
outgrower arrangements.
Sub-component 2.3: CCA rural finance policy dialogues
IFAD will be leading discussions on creating an enabling environment for climate finance with the participation of
ARACFIM financiers, the host bank, PFIs and value chain stakeholders. Links will be established for experience sharing
with private and public sector rural finance platforms and other green finance initiatives in the region.
25 See Financial Action Learning for Sustainability (FALS) https://gamechangenetwork.org/toolkit/fals-ifad-farmse-and-
opportunity-malawi/
26 Business development service providers will participate in the critical reflection and capacity building activities under 2.1.
27 Equity Bank uses International Labour Organisation (ILO) Start and Improve Your Business (SIYB) curricula

=== PAGE 108 ===
Page 104
This policy dialogue presents a unique opportunity to engage government and policy makers with GESI challenges
and opportunities linked to inclusive CCA finance. Engagement will be broadened to encourage collaboration and
exchange between government, NGOs and civil society on the nexus between GESI, climate change and financial
services. Key areas to address include28:
• Any gaps in the implementation of women’s land rights. Often gender equality in access to and control over
land is established at a constitutional level with relevant policy, legal and institutional reforms, but
implementation remains weak at sub-national levels as a result of a lack of knowledge, inability to access
relevant information, and contradictions with customary laws. Actions may be required to increase
understanding about the legal framework and strengthen implementation at the sub-national levels and
below.
• Women’s representation in advocacy, policy, and decision-making roles and processes. In 2019, the UNFCCC
recognized that the ‘full, meaningful and equal participation and leadership of women in in national- and
local-level climate policy and action is vital for achieving long-term climate goals”. However, women are
often under-represented on such bodies; likewise for the youth and people with disabilities. Their
participation can be increased by capacity strengthening programmes in confidence building and effective
public speaking, the use of quotas, and ensuring meeting times and venues are suitable. It may also be
necessary to engage with the traditional leadership on addressing any negative behavioural norms.
Implementation arrangements
The implementing structures for the GESI action plan through the host bank, Equity Bank Group, and the PFIs are
presented in Appendices 1 and 2.
The activities of Equity Bank Group and its Tier 2 Financial Intermediaries will be assessed based on their expected
alignment with national and international gender frameworks such as the Bloomberg Gender-Equality Index, 2X
Collaborative’s 2X Green toolkit, the Women Empowerment Principles (WEPs), the Gender Equality Mainstreaming
(GEM) Framework, and ‘A Fund Manager’s Guide to Gender-Smart Investing’ by IFC and CDC, and climate
frameworks such as GFANZ, TCFD, the CDSB Framework, Climate Action 100+, and the Just Transition criteria from
the Impact Investing Institute.
Programme management. Actions to strengthen the GESI dimensions of programme management:
• Support for GESI Action Plan implementation will be provided through the Technical Assistance, which
includes capacity building at different levels including for the Host Bank, PFIs and the end beneficiaries.
• Recruit a GESI specialist for the PMU at the Host Bank with responsibility to oversee: (i) the gender and
social norms diagnostic studies followed by the development of a detailed GESI action plan; (ii) capacity
strengthening and technical support to PMU staff and agents; (iii) the recruitment of experienced service
providers support gender mainstreaming and capacity building at the field level; (iv) liaison with the MEL
team; and (v) ongoing technical support during implementation.
• Include a specific commitment to gender-responsive financial inclusion in the terms of reference of the
programme manager, all staff in PMU and ARCAFIM focal points. This will be backed by capacity strengthening
on identifying and addressing gender issues. Annual performance reviews will track staff performance on
delivering GEWE and GESI goals.
28 Awiti, Op Cit

=== PAGE 109 ===
Page 105
• Ensure gender considerations are mainstreamed into all programme components.
• Select implementing partners (PFIs and other service providers) with a proven commitment to poverty
reduction and gender equality, and staff with the relevant GESI skills. The PFIs will be held accountable for
delivering on GESI targets.
• Seek opportunities to work with the range of regional and national organizations supporting the growth of
women as producers and entrepreneurs (see Appendix 3).
• Ensure that budgets are prepared, delivered and tracked from a gender perspective, and that the funds
allocated to implement gender-related activities are appropriate.
• Ensure the PMU workplace facilities, processes and policies are gender-responsive, supporting: gender parity
in recruitment, promotion, and retention of staff; equal pay between women and men for the same grade of
employment; life-work balance; policies and communications on the prevention of sexual exploitation,
discrimination, harassment and the abuse of authority.29
• Ensure the systematic integration of GESI considerations – including attention to representation and expertise
– in the programme’s governance, operational procedures, technical expert advisory bodies and decision-
making structures.
Gender and social norms diagnostic studies and detailed design of the GESI strategy. In order to increase the
financial inclusion of women, youth and people with disabilities and facilitate their economic empowerment,
diagnostic studies30 will be undertaken to understand how gender and social norms influence their financial
inclusion31 (or exclusion) and their ability to adapt to climate change.
Based on this understanding, activities to guide critical reflection will be delivered to seek positive behaviour change
at multiple levels – from the individual, household and community levels to PFIs, service providers and policymakers.
These will be coupled with intentional efforts to address key demand-and supply-side barriers which block people’s
ability to participate fully in CCA financial inclusion initiatives.
The studies will be context specific and conducted in each participating country. They will complement the standard
GESI analysis which examines the symptoms of gender inequality, youth, disability and poverty issues.32 The findings

Norms in Financial Inclusion – Diagnostic Guidance.” CGAP Background Document. Washington, DC: FinDev Gateway.

32 A GESI analysis typically examines inequalities in the areas of: access and control over resources and benefits; skills;
workloads; decision-making and leadership; household dynamics; and well-being. It describes relevant national policies,
strategies and actors; identifies the different roles, interests and priorities of women and men; and identifies the most
important livelihood problems and opportunities faced by the community, as experienced by women, men, youth and people
with disabilities. It also takes account of the differentiated and intersectional challenges facing different groups (for example, by
sex, age, marital status, socio-economic status, health, disability).

=== PAGE 110 ===
Page 106
will help shape the detailed GESI strategy to ensure that the design of CCA technologies and practices, financial
services and delivery channels will lead to more effective interventions to increase CCA financial inclusion.
Capacity building and behaviour change communication for PMU and key implementing partners. Following the
gender and social norms diagnostic studies, capacity development sessions will be undertaken with programme
staff, government, partners and service providers (in addition to those noted under component 2.1) to initiate critical
reflection on deep-rooted social and gender norms and attitudes, and their linkages with behaviours, in order to
bring about a paradigm shift towards women and CCA financial inclusion.33
Monitoring, evaluation and learning. To strengthen the GESI dimensions of MEL:
• Ensure all data in the logframe are disaggregated by sex, age and disability, where appropriate (including targets
and data on youth34).
• Train staff in the collection, analysis and interpretation of disaggregated data at various levels: from the
communities, PFIs and other service providers, to ARCAFIM focal points at country subsidiaries, PMU at host
bank HQ and ARCAFIM Facility Coordination Unit at IFAD. Use findings from data analysis to inform programme
management and activities.
• Track performance indicators, including by sex, age, location of the borrower, loan purpose, loan disbursements
and outstanding balances, as well as repayment performance.
• Establish and monitor mechanisms for tracking progress on outreach targets between the different levels of
programme management and service providers.
• Monitor and analyse beneficiary outreach, paying particular attention to ensuring the average value of loans to
women and youth are commensurate with those given to men for similar operations in the food system35, with
recommendations for remedial actions if required to strengthen outreach.
• Assess progress on GEWE and GESI through use of the project level index for the Women’s Empowerment in
Agriculture Index (Pro-WEAI36) or IFAD empowerment index. Monitor changes in social norms, gender roles and
power dynamics.
• Undertake studies on good practices leading to gender-responsive CCA financial inclusion outcomes and
impacts and organize knowledge management and sharing events to disseminate findings.
• Ensure GESI issues and financial inclusion actions are addressed during supervision missions and mid-term
review. Make provision for additional TA if it is recognised that the programme is failing to deliver on GESI
targets during the mid-term review.
33 For example, Care’s Social Analysis and Action methodology facilitates dialogue, enabling critical thinking and reflection
about how social conditions perpetuate food and nutrition insecurity and development challenges. https://www.care.org/our-
work/health/strengthening-healthcare/social-analysis-and-action-saa/
34 The youth target of 30% of beneficiaries needs to be disaggregated by sex; that is, 50% of the youth target is young women.
35 It is important to ensure that: (i) the targets for women and youth are met through their substantive engagement in
programme activities, such as entrepreneurs, not solely as minor beneficiaries, such as employees in an agribusiness; (ii)
women and youth have a proportionate share of the higher value loans and are not disproportionally clustered at the lower
end; and (iii) the funds available for MSMEs are not totally consumed by the larger enterprises.
36 Pro-WEAI is a composite index, which is based on 12 indicators of women’s empowerment in agriculture: autonomy in
income, self-efficacy, attitudes about domestic violence, input in productive decisions, ownership of land and other assets,
access to and decisions on credit, control over use of income, work balance, visiting important locations, group membership,
membership in influential groups, and respect among household members.

ARCAFIM GENDER AND SOCIAL INCLUSION ACTION STRATEGY
Activities Indicators & Targets Timeline Responsibilities Costs
Impact Statement: The ARCAFIM Program will seek to deploy innovative financial products along with Technical
Assistance to scale up CCA finance through the local private financial sector in selected East African (EA) countries
to address the unmet needs of rural producers involved in MSME food systems. ARCAFIM will mobilize US$ 270
million in CCA investments and $ 30 million in TA to enable vulnerable communities, including women and girls, to
identify, mitigate and adapt or increase their resilience to the negative impacts of climate change. Concurrently,
ARCAFIM will seek to create opportunities to enhance livelihoods, particularly for women and youth. In ensuring
equitable access to the benefits of the project, including employment and income generating activities, ARCAFIM
will identify the potential impact and technical assistance where possible, to support disenfranchised groups,
including women, to access climate resiliency tools and knowledge products to facilitate climate-smart
innovation. This will be done through the stakeholder engagement articulated in Annex 7 of the ARCAFIM funding
proposal and the capacity building provided under the Technical Assistance. ARCAFIM will enable local businesses,
including women-owned and/or led enterprises to adapt their existing methods, expand local markets and
demand, as well as attract further investment, especially for yet to be identified adaptation products and services.
Outcome Statement: ARCAFIM will secure climate change-resilient livelihoods with the benefits of food and
water security (including reduced workloads associated with household tasks); improved business and
employment opportunities and incomes; gains in voice and decision-making over the use of assets and outputs,
including income generated; and reductions in harmful practices and domestic violence. ARCAFIM will report
sex-disaggregated program level impacts to the extent possible and ensure that end beneficiary vulnerable
groups are protected against harmful practices such as child labour and exploitation, in order to meet both
national and International Labour Organization legal requirements. ARCAFIM will also achieve the following
gender-responsive outcomes:
1. Sustainable communities: ARCAFIM shall play a critical role in providing an avenue to collectively target
and empower women/youth/IPs, build on their agency and operationalize this GESI Action strategy,
through strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes as well as providing
access to women, youth and IP associations and village groups in East Africa. The organizations will in
turn provide access to training, collaborative efforts on tackling different aspects of climate adaptation
such as land planning, agro-forestry, soil conservation practices and energy saving stoves and technology.
A more detailed list of organizations can be accessed in Annex 4.
2. More balanced control over the four Factors of Production: ARCAFIM shall play a crucial role in promoting
a balance of control between men and women program end beneficiaries over the factors of production,
thereby facilitating their participation in decision-making processes.
3. Mitigate the disproportionate impact of climate change on vulnerable groups (women and young girls):

=== PAGE 112 ===
Page 108
Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people and communities in East
Africa via opportunities benefiting women and girls, leads to their enhanced access to resources and
economic engagement. Examples of these opportunities include access to financial resources, including
credit, to increase their economic power, and promotion of equal pay for equal work.
4. Increase the climate resilience and adaptation capacity for vulnerable communities, including women,
girls and indigenous communities in EA: via tools to support them in the adoption of distributed, data-
based, and efficient water technologies, and more resilient farming practices, in order to enhance access
to reliable and safe water supply, despite climate shocks and stresses.
5. Mainstreaming gender in concessional finance for climate change adaptation and related technical
advice: Apply a gender and climate lens across the investment process and ARCAFIM implementing
activities of Equity Bank Group and the Participating Financial Intermediaries (e.g. KWFT, Faulu, Rafiki,
TADB), via Gender and Climate Policies, as well as a Gender-Smart Climate Financing Toolkit to be used by
the bank to disburse direct and indirect concessional finance for climate adaptation and gender-smart
technical advice to smallholders and rural micro-, small- and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) in the
agricultural sector in East Africa.
The overall budget required for the implementation of the GSIAP is estimated as USD 960,000. This amount is
mostly included in the TA capacity building budget with the remainder in the Monitoring and Evaluation budget of
ARCAFIM.

=== PAGE 131 ===

ANNEX 3: Organisations Supporting Women Entrepreneurs in East Africa
REGIONAL
Organisation Description
Eastern Africa EAFF is a corporate members-based regional farmers’ organization founded in 2001. It is
Farmers registered in Kenya under the Societies Act, with country chapters in each of its member
Federation countries which includes all the ARCAFIM’s focus countries in East Africa i.e., Kenya,
(EAFF) Tanzania, Uganda and Rwanda. EAFF’s establishment was triggered by the development
imperatives of the late 1990s and early 2000, especially the need to strengthen mechanisms
for regional collective action to provide an opportunity for farmer organizations to engage
at the regional level. One of the organization’s core mandates includes active engagement
of women and youth in agricultural development regionally.
Africa Women AWAN Afrika is a non-profit organization limited by guarantee. Its network comprises
Agribusiness individual members’ businesses including producers, processors, aggregators, export
Network companies, and input suppliers among others across the continent and globally. The
(AWAN) Afrika organization provides women-owned and youth-owned agribusinesses with an E-Hub,

UN Women UN Women supports regional institutions, governments, and other stakeholders in setting
up and applying a comprehensive and dynamic set of global norms, policies and standards
on gender equality and empowerment of women.
Through policy and technical advisory services and regional engagement, the Regional
Office partners with regional bodies to support women’s participation in intergovernmental
policy processes such as the regional preparations for the Commission on the Status of
Women - CSW, the Rio+20 follow up, the Beijing+20 review, and the Post 2015
development agenda. These processes are linked to national level gender equality and
women empowerment advocacy.

build the gender-lens investing ecosystem for women climate entrepreneurs to grow
scalable climate-related businesses in Sub-Saharan Africa.

Kenya AWAK both at household and national levels.
Women Farmers WoFaAK is the national umbrella organization of women farmers in Kenya. WoFaAK is a non-
Association of political, non-government and non-religious member-based organization. The association
Kenya (WoFaAK) provides the women farmers of Kenya with a platform to articulate issues affecting them in
agriculture. The goal of WoFaAK is to address issues that impede women farmers from
undertaking both profitable and sustainable agricultural enterprises with a view to improve
their livelihoods.
Agriculture Agriculture Sector Network (ASNET) is the umbrella body representing the collective voice of
Sector Network the agriculture sector in Kenya. ASNET serves as the agriculture sector board for the Kenya
(ASNET) Private Sector Alliance (KEPSA).
ASNET’s key role is to coordinate agriculture sector actors in Kenya through various
mechanisms to engage in policy advocacy and value chain development that promote
increased productivity, competitiveness and attracts investments into the agriculture sector.
ASNET functions through a network of agencies and organisations such as AAK Grow,
Agricultural Employers Association, Agricultural Society of Kenya, Association of Kenya Feeds
Manufacturers (AKEFEMA), Avocado Society of Kenya, Cereal Growers Association (CGA),
Cereal Millers Association (CMA), Commercial Aquaculture Society of Kenya (CASK), East
African Tea Trade Association, Elgon Kenya Ltd, Kenya Association of Manufacturers (KAM),
Kenya Camel Association, Kenya Flower Council (KFC), Kenya National Farmers' Federation,
Kenya Tea Growers Association, Kenya Camel Association (KCA), Lake Naivasha Growers
Group, Micro Enterprises Support Programme Trust (MESPT), National Coffee Platform,
National Potatoes Council of Kenya, Seed Trade Association of Kenya and the Ustadi
Foundation.
The Kenya The Kenya National Farmers’ Federation (KENAFF) is the apex, umbrella farmers’
National organization in Kenya; also known as the National Farmers’ Organization (NFO). It was
Farmers’ founded in 1946 to represent, articulate, promote, and protect the interests of Kenyan
Federation through lobby, advocacy, policy action and farmer empowerment. The federation also
(KENAFF) deploys a wide range of member services:
Capacity building on organizational development for farmer groups/associations/enterprises.
Technical advisory and market facilitation.
Facilitating linkages between farmers and credit and finance institutions.
KENAFF’s membership comprises smallholder farmers organized in Producer Business
Groups (PBGs); County Farmers Associations (CFAs); apex Commodity Associations (CAs)
Cooperative Societies; Farmers’ Unions; farmer-owned business entities; and, large-scale
producers. The current membership is approximately 1.6 million farm families spread across
all the 47 counties and the eleven (11) KENAFF regions.

RWANDA
Organisation Description
Rwanda Rwanda Women’s Network was founded in 1995 to end gender inequality in Rwanda by
Women’s empowering women to be positive transformation agents in their homes, communities, and
Network the nation at large. One of the organization’s key pillars is socio-economic transformation
which focuses on enhancing access to knowledge and building financial skills as keys to the
mobilization of gender roles and economic growth in Rwanda. The organization’s programs
provide financial education and invest in the development of women led local small
businesses.
Rwanda Youth RYAF is a platform established to bring together different youth organizations, individual
in Agribusiness youth farmers and entrepreneurs in Agriculture Sector working in one or many of the
Forum (RYAF) following sub-sectors: crop production, livestock, agro-processing, inputs and other Agro-
services (extension, marketing, food packaging, farm mechanization, seed multiplication etc.)
as well as ICT for Agriculture in Rwanda. The platform aims to change the old mind-set
among the youth vis-à-vis the agricultural sector in Rwanda, while orienting youth to reach
out to other farming groups to raise awareness on the practice of business-oriented
agriculture.
Youth YEAN is a Youth-Led Agriculture social enterprise created to empower farmers and
Engagement in agripreneurs through capacity building, foster innovation, optimization of information
Agriculture availability, quality agriculture input access, efficient crop, and animal production
Network technologies deployment towards contributing to the SDG Goal 2: Ending hunger, achieving
(YEAN) food security, improving nutrition, and promoting sustainable agriculture through engaging
youth as agents of change.

UGANDA
Organisation Description
Support for SWAGEN is an indigenous organization founded and owned by grassroots women in
Women in Uganda. The organization came into existence in 1998 out of a felt need to go beyond
Agriculture and engaging women at the National level due to the proliferation of women focused NGOs at
Environment the national level. Its mission is to create strong women groups capable of active
(SWAGEN) participation in and meaningful contribution to mainstream national development.
SWAGEN advocates for a transition to agroecology as the food production model in Africa
as opposed to industrial agriculture. Agroecology is a people-centred system of sustainable
agriculture combining indigenous knowledge with cutting edge science, making the best
use of nature to create healthy communities. SWAGEN supports small-scale food producers
to build a sustainable, resilient, diverse, healthy, productive, and culturally appropriate
food system for Africa.
Association of AUPWAE is an association of professional women qualified and with vast experience in the
Uganda fields of Agriculture, Forestry, Fisheries, Food Science and Technology, Environmental
Professional Science and Veterinary Medicine. Founded in 1992, it currently boasts of a membership of
Women in 300 professional women working in both private and public sectors in all regions of the
Agriculture and country. One of their key objectives is to enhance the ability of rural women to acquire and
Environment utilize appropriate knowledge, technology and skills for improved food security, income
(AUPWAE) generation and environment conservation. The organization promotes training on
entrepreneurship skills and business planning, value chain development and
implementation, institutional capacity development, resource mobilization and in improved
agricultural and environment conservation technologies.
Rural Women Rural Women Empowerment Network (ROWONET) advances equity for women and girls
Empowerment throughout rural Uganda through research, advocacy, capacity building, and networking. A
Network membership organization comprising 179 women-led organizations across Uganda that
(ROWONET) represent the communities RUWONET serves, all members share a common vision:
equality and socio-economic justice for women and girls. Some of the key focus areas by
ROWONET include promoting climate smart agriculture, enhancing access to clean water
drinking water and promoting the use of renewable energy technologies in the rural areas.

ANNEX 6 The 2X Challenge Criteria
__________
